Devdiscourse News Desk | Bissau | Updated: 21-08-2026 09:51 IST | Created: 21-08-2026 09:51 IST UNICEF says more than US$150,000 is needed to support targeted parts of the mpox response in Guinea-Bissau. Image Credit: ChatGPT Country: Guinea-Bissau SHARE Key Takeaways AI Summary Guinea-Bissau is facing its first recorded mpox outbreak, with health authorities investigating dozens of suspected cases. Of the 46 suspected cases, 23 involve children under 15, raising concerns about the potential for serious complications. UNICEF is increasing support for public health efforts as officials work to identify infections and limit transmission. The upcoming school reopening in September poses additional challenges for managing the outbreak among children. Guinea-Bissau is confronting its first recorded mpox outbreak, with children emerging as a major concern as health authorities investigate dozens of suspected infections. Of the 46 suspected cases reported during the outbreak, 23 involve children younger than 15, prompting UNICEF to increase support for the country's public health response as officials work to identify infections and limit further transmission. The outbreak was declared on 4 July 2026. By 17 August, seven mpox cases had been confirmed, all among adults, with six recorded in the Autonomous Sector of Bissau and one in the Biombo Region. No deaths have been reported. The age profile of suspected infections has raised particular concern because 16 of the 23 children under 15 are between birth and four years old, an age group that can face a greater risk of serious complications from mpox. The figures do not mean these children have been confirmed to have the virus, but they underline the importance of rapid testing, medical assessment and close monitoring while health teams investigate the outbreak. September's school reopening adds another challenge. Children returning to classrooms and spending more time in close contact could create opportunities for transmission if infections are not identified quickly, making surveillance, family awareness and infection-prevention measures increasingly important in the weeks ahead. Unconnected cases raise fears of hidden transmission Health officials have found no apparent links between the confirmed cases, a pattern that suggests there may be transmission chains that surveillance teams have not yet detected. Guinea-Bissau's health surveillance system remains fragile, with important gaps in community-level case detection, contact identification, sample testing and systematic tracing of how infections are spreading. Sandra Martins, UNICEF Deputy Representative in Guinea-Bissau, described the country's first mpox outbreak as a significant public health event even though the number of confirmed infections remains relatively small. She warned that weak surveillance creates a risk that infections could go undetected, particularly among children who may be more vulnerable to complications. UNICEF expands support for the outbreak response UNICEF is working with Guinea-Bissau's Ministry of Public Health, the World Health Organization and other partners as part of the government-led response. Support includes strengthening disease surveillance and contact tracing, improving infection prevention and control in health facilities, reinforcing water, sanitation and hygiene measures, and helping families receive timely and accurate information about mpox. Public communication is especially important because recognising symptoms early can help people seek medical care sooner while reducing the chance of further spread. Families are being encouraged to seek care if a child or another household member develops symptoms such as a rash, fever or swollen lymph nodes, while following public health advice on handwashing and avoiding close contact with suspected cases. Health officials are also stressing the need to prevent stigma around people who become infected or are suspected of having mpox. Emergency funding needed to protect vulnerable families UNICEF says more than US$150,000 is needed to support targeted parts of the mpox response in Guinea-Bissau. Flexible emergency funding would allow critical resources to be deployed where they are most urgently required, particularly as health teams strengthen surveillance and prepare for the possibility of further infections. Early detection, appropriate care and practical prevention measures can help control mpox and reduce transmission. For Guinea-Bissau, the immediate challenge is finding infections that may currently be hidden while giving health workers, families and communities the information and resources needed to protect children during the country's first encounter with an mpox outbreak. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Colorado sees summer surge in Mpox infections - Denver7

News Brief
Colorado has experienced a summer surge in Mpox infections, with seven cases reported since late July and 18 cases recorded this year, compared to 21 in all of 2025. In Denver alone, six cases have been reported since June 1. The Denver Department of Public Health and Environment noted that these cases have no known connections or common exposures, though they can cluster in time. Most cases involve adult men, and no infections have been reported in children. Health officials recommend vaccination for individuals who have had close contact with confirmed cases or are at higher risk of exposure. They also highlighted that summer conditions and increased social interactions may create more opportunities for transmission. The situation is being closely monitored, with no specific prevention or treatment measures beyond vaccination recommendations.

By: Sophia Villalba Posted 3:57 PM, Aug 23, 2026 and last updated 3:57 PM, Aug 23, 2026 DENVER — Colorado is nearing last year's total number of Mpox cases after health officials reported a recent uptick in infections, including seven cases since late July. The Colorado Department of Public Health and Environment has recorded 18 cases this year, compared with 21 reported statewide in all of 2025. In Denver alone, the Denver Department of Public Health and Environment (DDPHE) has seen six cases since June 1. “The recent Denver cases have no known connections or common exposures, although cases can cluster in time,” Denver Department of Public Health and Environment public information officer Ryann Money said. 1/1 Skip Ad Continue watchingafter the adVisit Advertiser websiteGO TO PAGE DDPHE said that over the last few years, it's seen trends toward seasonal increases in spring and summer. “There are likely several factors that could contribute to an increase this time of year, like travel, which may create more opportunities for exposure and transmission,” Money told Denver7. Mpox, formerly known as monkeypox, can cause a rash or lesions, swollen lymph nodes, fatigue and muscle aches. The virus most commonly spreads through prolonged close contact, including direct contact with infectious skin lesions. “The rash or lesions that you would see on the skin from Mpox can last anywhere from several days to two weeks. In rare cases, even longer,” ER physician and KaufCare Advanced Urgent Care owner Noah Kaufman said. Denver7 ER physician and KaufCare Advanced Urgent Care owner Noah Kaufman Kaufman said summer conditions and increased social interaction can create more opportunities for transmission. “Right now, we see a lot of sweating. It's really hot out. There's a lot of close contact, and that's really how this bug is transmitted,” Kaufman said. State health officials said most Colorado cases this year have involved adult men, and no infections have been reported in children. The agency recommends vaccination for people who have had close contact with a confirmed case or who may be at increased risk of exposure. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

非洲航空公司助力抗击埃博拉、猴痘等疾病——非洲疾控中心 - 《太阳报》尼日利亚

African airlines have helped tackle Ebola, Mpox, others –CDC - The Sun Nigeria
新闻简报非洲疾控中心(Africa CDC)已认可非洲航空公司在抗击埃博拉、猴痘等疾病中的关键作用。在2026年8月非洲航空协会(AFRAA)SkyConnect领导力对话期间,Yap Boum II教授强调,可靠的空中连通性对于检测和控制这些疾病至关重要。他指出,若无正常运行的航班,国际卫生工作者将无法抵达疫区,导致疾病肆意扩散。Yap教授还强调了公共卫生部门与航空业合作的重要性,并表示应与社区共同开展应对工作,而非替代社区。他警告称,边境关闭可能导致各国因社会经济影响而隐瞒未来疫情。非洲疾控中心与受影响国家合作制定事件行动计划,确保协调应对。肯尼亚航空公司和埃塞俄比亚航空公司等航空公司在维持航空旅行的同时实施健康安全措施,被视为疾病管理的关键因素。应对工作仍在进行中,非洲疾控中心正致力于完善机构架构以提升疾病控制能力。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026年8月21日,对话活动于2026年8月AFRAA SkyConnect领导力对话期间举行。
地点与对象
非洲,肯尼亚航空公司和埃塞俄比亚航空公司被点名为疾病应对中的关键参与者。
事件
非洲航空公司被非洲疾控中心认定为疾病应对与控制的重要合作伙伴,特别是针对埃博拉、猴痘等疾病。该事件已由非洲疾控中心声明及Yap教授的讲话确认。
规模、影响与风险
非洲疾控中心强调空中连通性在检测、应对及控制疾病中的重要性。若航班运行中断,国际卫生工作者和专家将无法抵达疫区,导致疾病肆意扩散。疾病传播风险已被确认,但未进行量化。
应对、状态与不确定性
非洲疾控中心正与受影响国家合作,依据《国际卫生条例》制定事件行动计划。应对工作仍在进行中,空中连通性与筛查系统的有效性正作为控制策略的一部分进行讨论。信息来源状态完整,基于非洲疾控中心的官方声明。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

African airlines have helped tackle Ebola, Mpox, others –CDC - The Sun Nigeria

News Brief
The Africa CDC has acknowledged the critical role of African airlines in tackling outbreaks such as Ebola and monkeypox. During the August 2026 AFRAA SkyConnect Leadership Dialogues, Prof. Yap Boum II highlighted that reliable air connectivity is essential for detecting and containing these diseases. He noted that without functioning flights, international health workers would be unable to reach affected areas, allowing outbreaks to spread unchecked. Prof. Yap also emphasized the importance of collaboration between public health authorities and the aviation industry, stating that responses should be conducted with communities rather than for them. He warned that border closures could lead to countries concealing future outbreaks due to socio-economic impacts. The Africa CDC works with affected nations to create incident action plans, ensuring a coordinated response. The role of airlines, including Kenya Airways and Ethiopian Airlines, in maintaining air travel while implementing health safety measures was cited as a key factor in outbreak management. The response remains ongoing, with the Africa CDC focusing on institutional structures to improve outbreak containment.
Original source text (English)
Sunday, August 23, 2026 Follow Us on Google Search Home National Columns Business China News Politics & Power Opinion Entertainment The Sun TV Sporting Sun The Sun Foundation African airlines have helped tackle Ebola, Mpox, others –CDC 21 August 2026 4:17 am WAT By Chinelo Obogo Follow Us on Google The Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention (Africa CDC) has lauded African airlines for playing a key role in the continent’s response to disease outbreaks. It said the carriers have consistently served as important partners in disease surveillance and containment. Speaking at the August 2026 AFRAA SkyConnect Leadership Dialogues on Wednesday, the Head of Emergency Preparedness and Response Division at Africa CDC, Prof. Yap Boum II, said reliable air connectivity remains critical to Africa’s ability to detect, respond to and contain outbreaks such as Ebola and monkeypox, which continue to pose serious threats to public health and economic stability. Prof. Yap identified African carriers, including Kenya Airways and Ethiopian Airlines, as being at the forefront of outbreak response efforts. He said without functioning flight operations, international health workers and experts would be unable to reach affected regions, allowing outbreaks to spread unchecked. According to him, Kenya Airways and Ethiopian Airlines have introduced QR code-based screening systems at their hubs to keep air travel running as normally as possible while maintaining the necessary health safety measures. He said: “If you don’t have the plane, the flight, you won’t come and support, and then the outbreak will keep on growing. As we talk about solidarity, the airline companies play a critical role in ensuring that the support reaches where it’s supposed to be, but also in controlling that the outbreak is not spread across the other countries.” He spoke against countries that closed their borders in order to contain the outbreak, describing such measures as counterproductive and dangerous. He warned that when countries face punitive responses from the international community such as border closures to other countries that have declared outbreaks, then they would be incentivised to conceal any future ones. “If everyone closes the borders, as we have many, a few actually, some international countries that are closing borders to African countries, what will happen? The next time that a country will have an outbreak, they are not going to disclose, because they will pursue all the socio-economic impact that they will face. They will keep it, they will hide it, but if it’s open, then they know that as a matter of solidarity, all the countries will come and support,” he warned. He argued that keeping the air space open, ensuring robust screening at points of entry, is a far more effective strategy than a blanket ban on travel. He cited the case of a traveller who carried Ebola from the Democratic Republic of Congo to France, saying: “It was well-managed, and after that there were no cases. So it’s not because you have one case in one country that it’s the end of everything. All countries need to be prepared, proper screening. One day there’s a case, they manage it.” On understanding how monkeypox and ebola are transmitted, Prof. Yap offered explanations to why eliminating the animal sources of these diseases is not a good containment strategy. Other News Benue: Govt dispels rumours of Mpox outbreak Lagos launches ‘Operation BioShield’ campaign against Ebola threat Ebola: Lagos launches public awareness campaign He explained that while bats are believed to be a likely reservoir for Ebola and rodents such as squirrels for monkeypox, the exact animal reservoirs are uncertain and this makes targeted elimination impossible. His words: “We don’t know for sure which animals are the reservoir for those diseases. We believe that for Ebola, it could be bats. For monkeypox, it could be those squirrels. But if you are not sure, it means that you will keep all those animals in the zoo. It is just impossible.” He further explained how human activity is driving closer contact with wildlife, making zoonotic spillover inevitable, and highlighted how Mpox has dangerously evolved: “We had that mutation that increased the transmission from human to human. So that’s why we moved from the clade 1-E to the clade 1-B, and the clade 2-A to 2-B, where now you have the sexual transmission, which moved faster. And even for Ebola, the transmission is now human to human.” On the need for better collaboration, Prof. Yap acknowledged that there is a gap in communication between public health authorities and operators, stating that airlines are often left out of the conversation and plans until a crisis is already unfolding. He argued that the response to the outbreak must be conducted with communities and the aviation industry. He said: “The response to outbreaks should not be done for the community. It has to be done with the community, which means the information should really flow to ensure that everyone is aware. The traveller should actually be the one to ask, where is your camera? I know that there is an outbreak somewhere, so why are you not screening? So this communication is also emphasising that the response to outbreaks is not a medical matter, it’s also a communication matter. “We take time to come and have that dialogue, because we know that a large part of the population, which is not medical, they need to have the information, they need to know what is happening, they need to know that all that is being done, it has to be done with them and not for them. That’s, for us, really critical.” With regards to the framework of Africa CDC’s, he explained that at the continental level, the CDC activates its coordination role when an outbreak crosses national borders. He said under the International Health Regulations, countries are required to notify the World Health Organisation (WHO) and Africa CDC as soon as a significant outbreak is detected. Once these organisations are notified, Africa CDC works with affected countries to establish an incident action plan which covers the immediate, medium, and long-term response timelines. On the question of which institution takes precedence during an outbreak, Prof. Yap said: “Countries have agreed to follow them, and then when the time comes to have the plan, the country will decide which plan fits for the environment, which response fits to the culture, to the context. At the end of the day, the community has the lead, the country has the lead, and all the partners now will come to support where there are gaps based on the vision of the country. “The vision of Africa CDC is Africa health security and sovereignty. It means that countries lead, and all the multilateral organizations support. We come with our conception on how to respond, and the community is telling us that no, no, no, no, that’s not how we want you to support us.” Prof. Yap acknowledged that the risk of outbreak spreading can never be completely eliminated, but maintained that strong institutional structures can contain outbreaks at their source. He highlighted the importance of National Public Health Institutes, saying: “Previously, the way we were responding to outbreaks, when there is an outbreak, you create one commission. You have a chair, you have the members, and then after the outbreak, they disappear. So, there are no institutional memories on how to respond. So with the NPHI, you bring the epidemiologies, you bring the laboratory, so you have that structure. “We keep on strengthening and strengthening, to the extent that when an outbreak comes, because that one cannot fully avoid, at least they can control it at a source. The risk is zero. It doesn’t exist. It can happen. You have a case that went from the DRC to France. It was well-managed, and after that there were no cases. So it’s not because you have one case in one country that it’s the end of everything. All countries need to be prepared”, he stated. Guinea-Bissau Reports First Mpox Outbreak, UNICEF Warns of Risks to Children - DNE Africa

News Brief
Guinea-Bissau has reported its first mpox outbreak, according to a news article published on August 20, 2026. The report, issued by DNE Africa, highlights concerns raised by UNICEF about the risks this disease poses to children, given the country's fragile disease surveillance system. The outbreak is described as the initial occurrence of mpox in the nation, and the situation is under investigation. No specific case counts, death numbers, or exposure details are provided in the evidence. The article underscores the importance of strengthening public health infrastructure to manage the outbreak effectively. The report does not mention any preventive measures or treatment protocols, focusing instead on the risks and the need for further action.

The situation is under investigation, with the report noting the need for improved disease surveillance and highlighting the risks to children.

几内亚比绍报告首例 猴痘 暴发,疑似病例中儿童占一半——《今日印度》

Guinea-Bissau Reports First Mpox Outbreak As Children Make Up Half Of Suspected Cases - Times Now
新闻简报截至2026年8月20日,几内亚比绍报告了首例 猴痘 暴发,共确诊7例,疑似病例46例正在调查中,其中23例为15岁以下儿童。联合国儿童基金会强调,儿童占疑似病例的一半,并对该国脆弱的医疗系统发出警告。联合国儿童基金会副代表Sandra Martins表示,暴发因其对脆弱人群的潜在影响而成为一项重大公共卫生事件。本次 暴发 中尚无死亡病例报告。非洲疾病预防控制中心指出,猴痘 在多个地区仍属地方性流行,几内亚比绍的情况正在密切监测中。下月学校重新开放可能增加儿童间的 传播 机会,引发疾病传播的担忧。
查看新闻五要素
时间
几内亚比绍于2026年8月20日报告首例 猴痘 暴发。该 暴发 于2026年8月17日确认,共确诊7例。疑似病例截至2026年8月20日正在调查中。

本次 暴发 发生在几内亚比绍,该国位于西非。受影响人群包括儿童,15岁以下疑似病例达23例。
事件
几内亚比绍出现首例 猴痘 暴发,疑似病例正在调查中,确诊病例已报告。联合国儿童基金会强调儿童在疑似病例中的作用,并对医疗系统脆弱性表达关切。
规模、影响与风险
确诊病例7例,疑似病例46例正在调查中。目前无死亡病例报告。由于医疗系统脆弱且儿童面临重症并发症风险,本次 暴发 被视为重大公共卫生事件。

Guinea-Bissau Reports First Mpox Outbreak As Children Make Up Half Of Suspected Cases - Times Now

News Brief
Guinea-Bissau has reported its first mpox outbreak as of August 20, 2026, with seven confirmed cases and 46 suspected cases under investigation, including 23 children under 15. UNICEF highlighted that children make up half of the suspected cases and warned about the fragile health surveillance system in the country. The deputy representative of UNICEF, Sandra Martins, stated that the outbreak is a major public health event due to its potential impact on vulnerable populations. No deaths have been reported in the current outbreak. The Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention noted that mpox remains endemic in several regions, and the situation in Guinea-Bissau is being closely monitored. The reopening of schools next month could increase transmission opportunities among children, raising concerns about the spread of the disease.

Times NowTimes Now NavbharatZoomET NowET Now SwadeshET Now AdvisorTimes DriveHealth and MeDigitMarathiTeluguTamilBengaliKannadaMalayalamUnilist Times DriveHealth and MeDigitMarathiTeluguTamilBengaliKannadaMalayalamUnilist Live TV World US UK Europe Asia Middle East Australia Canada Featured US News US Buzz Search Latest India Cities Mumbai Delhi Kolkata Bengaluru Hyderabad Chennai Pune Crime World US US News US Buzz UK Europe Asia Middle East Australia Canada Entertainment Bollywood South OTT TV Hollywood Reviews Korean Sports Cricket Hockey World Cup Football Pickleball WWE Tennis Badminton Athletics Padel Basketball Live Score NBA Business Personal Finance Markets Companies Economy Lifestyle Relationships Fashion Books People Food Travel Opinion Pickleball Crypto Videos India World Shows Newshour India Upfront Nation Wants To Know Frankly Speaking Specials Digit Viral Video Elections Education Tech Weather Web Stories Elections LOT Health Auto Car News Bike News Electric Vehicles Features Viral Opinion World India Spirituality Astrology Photos US News Guinea-Bissau Reports First Mpox Outbreak As Children Make Up Half Of Suspected Cases Authored by: Samannay Biswas Updated Aug 20, 2026, 23:25 IST Guinea-Bissau is experiencing its first mpox outbreak, with UNICEF highlighting that children make up half of the suspected cases. “This is the first time mpox cases have been reported in Guinea-Bissau, making even a small outbreak a major public health event,” said Sandra Martins, UNICEF’s deputy representative in the country. follow usfollow us Guinea-Bissau is facing its first reported mpox outbreak, with UNICEF warning that children account for half of the suspected cases and that the West African country’s fragile health surveillance system may struggle to detect further infections. UNICEF said Thursday that the country had reported seven confirmed mpox cases as of August 17, all involving adults. However, 46 suspected cases were under investigation, including 23 children below the age of 15. UNICEF Raises Concern Over Children “This is the first time mpox cases have been reported in Guinea-Bissau, making even a small outbreak a major public health event,” said Sandra Martins, UNICEF’s deputy representative in the country. She warned that Guinea-Bissau’s disease surveillance system remains “extremely fragile”, raising concerns that infections could go undetected. “Cases may be going undetected, particularly among children who are the most vulnerable to severe complications,” Martins said. UNICEF also cautioned that the reopening of schools next month could increase opportunities for transmission among children. What Is Mpox? Mpox is caused by a virus belonging to the same family as smallpox. It can spread from animals to humans and can also be transmitted between people through close physical contact. The disease was first identified in humans in 1970 in the Democratic Republic of Congo. Common symptoms can include fever, muscle aches and characteristic skin lesions. In some cases, the infection can become severe or fatal. Guinea-Bissau Faces Preparedness Challenges The emergence of mpox in Guinea-Bissau is particularly concerning because of the country's limited capacity to monitor and respond to infectious disease outbreaks. UNICEF said the country needs stronger surveillance and greater preparedness to identify cases quickly, particularly among vulnerable children. No deaths have been reported in the current outbreak so far. The Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention said in January that Africa was no longer under a continent-wide public health emergency over mpox, although the virus remains endemic in several settings. The situation in Guinea-Bissau is now being closely monitored as authorities assess suspected cases and prepare for the potential spread of the disease. 猴痘 是否会长期存在?CDC 研究称该病毒可能正在成为地方性流行

Is mpox here to stay? CDC study says virus is likely becoming endemic
新闻简报美国疾病控制与预防中心(CDC)研究显示,猴痘 可能正在美国成为地方性流行病,持续低水平的病毒活动表明该病毒正在持续传播。始于 2022 年的全球 暴发 已在 122 个国家感染 100,000 人,其中包括 115 个此前未报告 猴痘 的地区。在美国,猴痘 最常见于男男性行为者人群;2024 年和 2025 年,76% 的病例发生在未接种疫苗的个体中,这些未接种者住院风险近乎 10 倍。CDC 建议高风险人群(包括前往受影响国家的旅行者)接种两剂 Jynneos 疫苗。该研究强调需持续开展 监测 并采取公共卫生措施以控制 猴痘 的传播,该病毒主要通过密切接触(如拥抱、亲吻或性行为)传播。CDC 还建议如出现腹泻、发热或脱水等症状,应及时就医。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Is mpox here to stay? CDC study says virus is likely becoming endemic

News Brief
The CDC study suggests that mpox is likely becoming endemic in the United States, with persistent, low levels of the virus indicating continuous circulation. The global outbreak, which began in 2022, has infected 100,000 people in 122 countries, including 115 where mpox was not previously reported. In the US, mpox is most common among men who have sex with men, and in 2024 and 2025, 76% of cases were in unvaccinated individuals, who were nearly 10 times more likely to be hospitalized. The CDC recommends two doses of the Jynneos vaccine for those at risk, including travelers to affected countries. The study highlights the need for ongoing surveillance and public health measures to manage the spread of mpox, which spreads through close contact such as cuddling, kissing, or sex. The CDC also advises seeking medical care if symptoms like diarrhea, fever, or dehydration occur.

CDC study says virus is likely becoming endemic News brief Liz Szabo, MA Topics Mpox Share Copied to clipboard Colorized transmission electron micrograph of mpox virus particles (blue) cultivated and purified from cell culture. NIAID/NIH Although mpox cases related to the 2022 global outbreak were diagnosed in people who had traveled aboard, the virus now is spreading continuously in the United States. Persistent, low levels of mpox “suggest a likely transition toward endemic circulation,” according to a report in the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention’s (CDC’s) flagship publication, Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report. The paper also quantifies the risk of hospitalization in unvaccinated mpox patients. Mpox spreads through close contact, such as cuddling, kissing, or sex. Mpox can spread among household contacts, either from skin-to-skin contact or by sharing towels or bedding. In the current outbreak in Guinea-Bissau, half of cases are in children. In the United States, mpox is most common among men who have sex with men. The CDC recommends that people at risk for mpox, including those traveling to countries with outbreaks of the virus, receive two doses of the Jynneos vaccine. Unvaccinated at much higher risk of hospitalization In 2024 and 2025, according to the report, 76% of mpox cases in the United States were in unvaccinated people, who were nearly 10 times as likely to be hospitalized with the virus as those who received both vaccine doses. Before 2022, US mpox cases were related to foreign travel or infections from small animals imported from abroad. There are two types of mpox, clade 1 and clade 2. The CDC confirmed about 30,000 clade 2b mpox cases in the United States in 2022. The global outbreak of clade 2b mpox that began in 2022 has infected 100,000 people in 122 total countries, including 115 countries in which mpox was not previously reported. FDA, CDC investigating multistate outbreak of E coli, Salmonella linked to sprouts News brief Meghan Holohan Topics Salmonella E coli Foodborne Disease Erin Collins / Flickr cc Today the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) and the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) announced they are investigating an outbreak of Shiga toxin–producing Escherichia coli and Salmonella linked to alfalfa sprouts that has sickened 55 people, sending four people to the hospital, in 15 states. Trace-back investigation points to alfalfa sprouts State health departments and the CDC confirmed 46 people infected with E coli, seven people infected with Salmonella, and two people having both E coli and Salmonella infections. People became ill from May 31 through August 8. About three-fourths of people interviewed—26 of 34 (76%)—said they ate alfalfa sprouts, and trace-back investigations identified alfalfa sprouts distributed by Everything Sprouts as the potential source of this outbreak. On Wednesday, the FDA started collecting samples from the company. The FDA recommends that Everything Sprouts recall its product and urges people to avoid eating sprouts sold under the Calco or Everything Sprouts brand. People with these products should toss them out or return them to the store where they were purchased. States with cases are Florida, Indiana, Iowa, Kansas, Michigan, Minnesota, New Hampshire, New York, North Carolina, North Dakota, Pennsylvania, South Carolina, South Dakota, Washington, and Wisconsin. Minnesota has reported the most cases: 21. Both E coli and Salmonella cause diarrhea and stomach cramps. E coli can also lead to vomiting and bloody stool, while Salmonella can cause fever. The CDC recommends people seek medical care if they have any of these symptoms: diarrhea and a fever higher than 102°F, bloody diarrhea, diarrhea that lasts for more than two days and hasn’t improved, vomiting that impairs the ability to keep fluids down, and dehydration. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Guinea-Bissau faces first mpox outbreak: UNICEF - TRT Afrika

News Brief
Guinea-Bissau is experiencing its first mpox outbreak, as confirmed by UNICEF on August 20, 2026. The outbreak has resulted in seven confirmed cases, all among adults, with no reported deaths. However, 46 suspected cases have been identified, 23 of which involve children under 15. UNICEF has raised concerns about the risk of undetected cases, especially among children, and warned that the reopening of schools next month could exacerbate the spread of the disease. Mpox, a virus related to smallpox, is known to cause fever, muscular aches, and large skin lesions, and can be deadly. The Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention stated that mpox remains endemic in several regions, though it is no longer classified as a public health emergency. The situation is under active investigation, with efforts focused on monitoring and preventing further transmission.
Original source text (English)
| English | English POLITICSAFRICATÜRKİYEOPINIONFEATURESSPORTS AFRICA 2 min read Guinea-Bissau faces first mpox outbreak: UNICEF Guinea-Bissau is facing its first outbreak of mpox, UN children's agency UNICEF said on Thursday. Share Mpox can be transmitted to humans by animals but can also be passed between people. August 20, 2026 Guinea-Bissau is facing its first outbreak of mpox, UN children's agency UNICEF said on Thursday, voicing concern that half of suspected cases were affecting children. Mpox, which is caused by a virus from the same family as smallpox, can be transmitted to humans by animals but can also be passed between people through close physical contact. The disease, which was first detected in humans in 1970 in the Democratic Republic of Congo, causes fever, muscular aches and large boil-like skin lesions, and can be deadly. "This is the first time mpox cases have been reported in Guinea-Bissau, making even a small outbreak a major public health event," Sandra Martins, UNICEF deputy representative in Guinea-Bissau, said in a statement. Guinea-Bissau confirms seven cases RECOMMENDED Seven family members aboard migrant boat that sank off Tunisia's coast Kenyan police arrest four, recover toxic chemicals as Amboseli elephant deaths rise Sudan says it shot down 'hostile' drone in Blue Nile state DRC and M23 rebels agree roadmap for stalled peace talks RECOMMENDED Seven family members aboard migrant boat that sank off Tunisia's coast Kenyan police arrest four, recover toxic chemicals as Amboseli elephant deaths rise Sudan says it shot down 'hostile' drone in Blue Nile state DRC and M23 rebels agree roadmap for stalled peace talks "We face the risk that cases are going undetected, particularly among children who are the most vulnerable to severe complications," she said. As of August 17, Guinea-Bissau had reported seven confirmed mpox cases, all among adults. No deaths have been reported. But, out of 46 suspected cases, 23 are children aged under 15, UNICEF said, adding the reopening of schools next month could contribute to spread of the disease. The Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention announced in January that Africa was no longer facing a public health emergency over mpox though the viral infection "remains endemic in several settings." Children Make Up Half of Suspected Mpox Cases in Guinea-Bissau - Mirage News

News Brief
In Guinea-Bissau, a suspected mpox outbreak has been reported, with children accounting for half of the 46 suspected cases, 16 of whom are under the age of four. The outbreak, declared on 4 July, is considered a "major public health event" by UNICEF, marking the first mpox cases in the West African country. Seven adult cases have been confirmed by the Government, with no fatalities reported. Mpox is an infectious disease related to smallpox, and symptoms include a rash, fever, chills, and fatigue. The upcoming reopening of schools in September raises concerns about increased transmission among children, who are more vulnerable to severe cases. The World Health Organization (WHO) offers further information on mpox, but the full original article body was not available for review.

The seemingly minor outbreak represents a "major public health event" according to UN Children's Fund UNICEF , as this is the first time mpox cases have been reported in the West African country. Mpox is an infectious disease belonging to the same virus family as smallpox. Symptoms typically include a distinct rash, alongside fever, chills, swollen lymph nodes, muscle aches and fatigue. 几内亚比绍疑似猴痘病例中儿童占一半 - WebWire

Children account for half of suspected mpox cases in Guinea-Bissau - WebWire
新闻简报Guinea-Bissau is dealing with its first mpox outbreak, with 46 suspected cases reported as of 17 August 2026. Children under 15 make up half of these suspected cases, with 16 aged 0-4 years, who are at higher risk for severe complications. Seven confirmed cases have been reported, all among adults, and no deaths have occurred. The outbreak has prompted UNICEF to scale up its support to the Ministry of Public Health, working with WHO and other partners to improve surveillance, contact tracing, and infection prevention measures. The country’s surveillance system is described as extremely fragile, and there are concerns about undetected transmission chains. The reopening of schools in September could further increase transmission risks, especially among children. UNICEF has requested over US$150,000 to address critical intervention needs and protect vulnerable populations.
查看新闻五要素
时间
猴痘暴发于2026年7月4日被宣布,截至2026年8月17日已报告7例确诊猴痘病例。疑似病例报告时间为2026年8月17日。
地点与对象
几内亚比绍,具体为比绍自治区和比翁博地区。受影响人群包括15岁以下儿童,其中23例疑似病例,16例年龄为0-4岁。
事件
几内亚比绍正在经历首次猴痘暴发,报告46例疑似病例和7例确诊病例(均为成人)。15岁以下儿童占疑似病例的一半,引发对其重症并发症易感性的担忧。
规模、影响与风险
共有46例疑似猴痘病例,其中23例为15岁以下儿童。16名儿童年龄在0-4岁,属于高风险群体。目前尚无死亡病例报告,但强调了未被发现的传播链及儿童重症风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
UNICEF正在支持公共卫生部,并与WHO及其他伙伴合作,加强监测、接触者追踪和感染预防措施。该国监测系统被描述为极其脆弱,且存在未被发现的传播链隐患。9月学校重新开学预计将增加传播风险。

Children account for half of suspected mpox cases in Guinea-Bissau - WebWire

News Brief
Guinea-Bissau is dealing with its first mpox outbreak, with 46 suspected cases reported as of 17 August 2026. Children under 15 make up half of these suspected cases, with 16 aged 0-4 years, who are at higher risk for severe complications. Seven confirmed cases have been reported, all among adults, and no deaths have occurred. The outbreak has prompted UNICEF to scale up its support to the Ministry of Public Health, working with WHO and other partners to improve surveillance, contact tracing, and infection prevention measures. The country’s surveillance system is described as extremely fragile, and there are concerns about undetected transmission chains. The reopening of schools in September could further increase transmission risks, especially among children. UNICEF has requested over US$150,000 to address critical intervention needs and protect vulnerable populations.
Original source text (English)
News and Press Release Distribution, Since 1995 Deliver Your News to the World Sign In Create a Free Account Home News How We Work Compare Services FAQ All WebPost WebRelease Targeted Media Wire Service Children account for half of suspected mpox cases in Guinea-Bissau UNICEF strengthens surveillance, prevention, and community outreach as country responds to its first mpox outbreak Share LinkedIn Twitter Facebook Reddit Pinterest Email WhatsApp Messenger Telegram Gmail BISSAU/DAKAR – WEBWIRE – Thursday, August 20, 2026 UNICEF/UNI624811/Jospin Benekire. FILE PHOTO: A mother and her young child in treatment at the mpox isolation unit of the UNICEF-supported Kavumu hospital in South Kivu province, DR Congo, on 23 July 2024. Children under 15 account for half of all 46 suspected mpox cases in Guinea-Bissau. UNICEF is scaling up its support to the Ministry of Public Health in direct response to the country’s first-ever mpox outbreak. “This is the first time mpox cases have been reported in Guinea-Bissau, making even a small outbreak a major public health event,” said Sandra Martins, UNICEF Deputy Representative in Guinea Bissau. “While the country’s surveillance system remains extremely fragile, we face the risk that cases are going undetected, particularly among children who are the most vulnerable to severe complications.” Since the outbreak was declared on 4 July 2026, Guinea-Bissau has reported seven confirmed mpox cases, all among adults, as of 17 August. Six of the confirmed cases are in the Autonomous Sector of Bissau while one is in Biombo Region. No deaths have been reported to date. However, of the 46 suspected cases, 23 are children aged under 15. Sixteen of those children are aged 0-4 years, raising concerns for their health given the greater risk of complications from the virus among young children. The reopening of schools in September further compounds these concerns, given the potential for increased transmission among children. Confirmed cases do not appear connected to each other, suggesting undetected chains of transmission, and the possibility that additional cases may be going unidentified. Critical gaps remain in community-based surveillance, especially in identifying contacts, testing samples, and systematically tracing how the virus is spreading. UNICEF is working closely with the Ministry of Public Health, WHO, and partners to support the government-led response, strengthen surveillance and contact tracing, reinforce infection prevention control and water, sanitation and hygiene measures in health facilities, and ensure that children and families receive timely, and accurate and information to help prevent further spread of mpox. “With early detection, care, and prevention, mpox can be managed and its spread reduced,” said Sandra Martins. “We urge families to seek care immediately if a child or family member develops a rash, fever, swollen lymph nodes or other symptoms, and to follow guidance on handwashing, avoiding close contact with suspected cases, and reducing stigma.” While the broader national response is underway, UNICEF requires more than US$150,000 to support targeted, critical pillars of the mpox intervention in Guinea-Bissau. Flexible emergency funding is urgently required to deploy these vital resources and protect vulnerable populations. **** About UNICEF UNICEF, the United Nations agency for children, works to protect the rights of every child, everywhere, especially the most disadvantaged children and in the toughest places to reach. Across more than 190 countries and territories, we do whatever it takes to help children survive, thrive, and fulfil their potential. Mpox: Children account for half of suspected cases in Guinea-Bissau
新闻简报A recent mpox outbreak in Guinea-Bissau has raised concerns, with children accounting for half of the 46 suspected cases, 16 of whom are under four years old. The outbreak, declared on 4 July, is considered a 'major public health event' by UNICEF, as it marks the first mpox cases in the West African country. While seven adult cases have been confirmed, no fatalities have been reported. The disease spreads through skin-to-skin contact and contaminated objects, making children and newborns particularly vulnerable. UNICEF is collaborating with local authorities to improve surveillance and reduce transmission risks, emphasizing the need for immediate care if symptoms like rash, fever, or swollen lymph nodes appear. The agency is also seeking $150,000 in emergency funding to support these efforts. The upcoming school reopening in September is expected to increase transmission risks among children, prompting calls for hygiene education and stigma reduction.

Facebook Twitter Print Email Mpox: Children account for half of suspected cases in Guinea-Bissau 20 August 2026 Health Children, who are particularly vulnerable to the disease, account for half of the 46 suspected mpox cases identified in Guinea-Bissau – with 16 of them under the age of four. The seemingly minor outbreak represents a “major public health event” according to UN Children’s Fund UNICEF, as this is the first time mpox cases have been reported in the West African country. Mpox is an infectious disease belonging to the same virus family as smallpox. Symptoms typically include a distinct rash, alongside fever, chills, swollen lymph nodes, muscle aches and fatigue. Find more on mpox here, from the World Health Organization (WHO). The outbreak was declared on 4 July. Seven adult cases have been confirmed so far, according to the Government, as of Tuesday, with no fatalities reported. Back to school Symptoms of mpox tend to go away on their own and rarely lead to death, but newborns and children are more at risk of developing severe cases of the disease. Tweet URL In the last major international outbreak of mpox in 2022, many of the fatal cases were of children or people with underlying health conditions. The upcoming reopening of schools in September heightens concerns, as it is expected to increase opportunities for transmission among this vulnerable group. Close contact Though the outbreak of 2022 primarily affected people with new and/or multiple sexual partners – including men who have sex with men – there are many ways to get infected. The disease primarily spreads through skin-to-skin contact with lesions, so anyone who comes into close physical contact with a contagious person is at risk. Contact with objects used by the infected person – such as clothing and eating utensils – also represents an important source of infection. ‘Critical gaps’ in surveillance UNICEF warns that “critical gaps” in surveillance strategies – especially in identifying contacts, testing samples and tracing how the virus spread – may complicate efforts to address the outbreak. The agency is working with local authorities and partners to address them and organise information campaigns for children and families. These initiatives require more than $150,000 in flexible emergency funding. “With early detection, care, and prevention, mpox can be managed and its spread reduced,” stressed the UNICEF Deputy Representative Sandra Martins. “We urge families to seek care immediately if a child or family member develops a rash, fever, swollen lymph nodes or other symptoms, and to follow guidance on handwashing, avoiding close contact with suspected cases, and reducing stigma.” Mpox Facebook Twitter Print Email

几内亚比绍正经历其首次猴痘 暴发暴发,报告46例疑似病例及7例确诊病例(均为成人)。在疑似病例中,23例为15岁以下儿童,其中16名年龄为0-4岁,引发对幼儿重症风险的担忧。
规模、影响与风险
共报告46例疑似猴痘病例及7例确诊病例。目前无死亡病例报告。由于监测系统脆弱,儿童中存在大量未被发现病例的高风险。9月学校重新开放可能增加传播传播风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
暴发 正在调查中,联合国儿童基金会(UNICEF)正在支持公共卫生部与世界卫生组织(WHO)加强 监测、接触者追踪及感染预防措施。社区层面的 监测 仍存在关键缺口,需额外投入超过 150,000 美元用于针对性干预措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Half of Suspected Mpox Cases in Guinea-Bissau Are Kids - Mirage News

News Brief
Guinea-Bissau is facing its first-ever mpox outbreak, with 46 suspected cases reported as of 20 August 2026. Of these, 23 are children under 15, including 16 aged 0-4 years, who are at higher risk of severe complications. Seven confirmed cases have been reported, all among adults, as of 17 August. The country's surveillance system is described as extremely fragile, raising concerns that cases may be going undetected, particularly among children. The outbreak was declared on 4 July 2026, and the reopening of schools in September could further increase transmission risks. UNICEF is collaborating with the Ministry of Public Health and WHO to enhance surveillance, contact tracing, and infection prevention measures. No deaths have been reported to date, but the response requires over US$150,000 in emergency funding to address critical gaps in community-based monitoring and protect vulnerable populations.
Original source text (English)
Mirage News Mirage News Mirage News World 20 Aug 2026 11:24 pm AEST Date Time Discover more Science news updates News content API News media partnership Children under 15 account for half of all 46 suspected mpox cases in Guinea-Bissau. UNICEF is scaling up its support to the Ministry of Public Health in direct response to the country's first-ever mpox outbreak. "This is the first time mpox cases have been reported in Guinea-Bissau, making even a small outbreak a major public health event," said Sandra Martins, UNICEF Deputy Representative in Guinea Bissau. "While the country's surveillance system remains extremely fragile, we face the risk that cases are going undetected, particularly among children who are the most vulnerable to severe complications." Since the outbreak was declared on 4 July 2026, Guinea-Bissau has reported seven confirmed mpox cases, all among adults, as of 17 August. Six of the confirmed cases are in the Autonomous Sector of Bissau while one is in Biombo Region. No deaths have been reported to date. However, of the 46 suspected cases, 23 are children aged under 15. Sixteen of those children are aged 0-4 years, raising concerns for their health given the greater risk of complications from the virus among young children. The reopening of schools in September further compounds these concerns, given the potential for increased transmission among children. Confirmed cases do not appear connected to each other, suggesting undetected chains of transmission, and the possibility that additional cases may be going unidentified. Critical gaps remain in community-based surveillance, especially in identifying contacts, testing samples, and systematically tracing how the virus is spreading. UNICEF is working closely with the Ministry of Public Health, WHO, and partners to support the government-led response, strengthen surveillance and contact tracing, reinforce infection prevention control and water, sanitation and hygiene measures in health facilities, and ensure that children and families receive timely, and accurate and information to help prevent further spread of mpox. "With early detection, care, and prevention, mpox can be managed and its spread reduced," said Sandra Martins. "We urge families to seek care immediately if a child or family member develops a rash, fever, swollen lymph nodes or other symptoms, and to follow guidance on handwashing, avoiding close contact with suspected cases, and reducing stigma." While the broader national response is underway, UNICEF requires more than US$150,000 to support targeted, critical pillars of the mpox intervention in Guinea-Bissau. Flexible emergency funding is urgently required to deploy these vital resources and protect vulnerable populations. /Public Release. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Mpox outbreak in Guinea-Bissau sparks alarm: Children account for half of suspected cases, says UNICEF - Indiablooms

News Brief
A mpox outbreak in Guinea-Bissau has raised alarm, with children accounting for half of the 46 suspected cases, including 16 under the age of four. The outbreak, declared on 4 July, is the first reported in the West African country. While seven adult cases have been confirmed, no fatalities have been recorded. UNICEF has labeled this a major public health event, emphasizing the vulnerability of children and the need for improved surveillance. The agency is collaborating with local authorities to implement information campaigns and address gaps in contact tracing and testing. Concerns are heightened by the upcoming school reopening in September, which may increase transmission risks among children. UNICEF urges families to seek immediate care if symptoms like rash, fever, or swollen lymph nodes appear and to follow preventive measures such as handwashing and avoiding close contact with suspected cases.
Original source text (English)

vaccinates a child at a health centre in Quebo, Guinea-Bissau.Photo: UNICEF/Vincent Tremeau Mpox outbreak in Guinea-Bissau sparks alarm: Children account for half of suspected cases, says UNICEF India Blooms News Service | @indiablooms | Aug 21, 2026, at 09:47 am Children, who are particularly vulnerable to the disease, account for half of the 46 suspected mpox cases identified in Guinea-Bissau – with 16 of them under the age of four. FamilyLaw The seemingly minor outbreak represents a “major public health event” according to UN Children’s Fund UNICEF, as this is the first time mpox cases have been reported in the West African country. Mpox is an infectious disease belonging to the same virus family as smallpox. Symptoms typically include a distinct rash, alongside fever, chills, swollen lymph nodes, muscle aches and fatigue. The outbreak was declared on 4 July. Seven adult cases have been confirmed so far, according to the Government, as of Tuesday, with no fatalities reported. Back to school Symptoms of mpox tend to go away on their own and rarely lead to death, but newborns and children are more at risk of developing severe cases of the disease. In the last major international outbreak of mpox in 2022, many of the fatal cases were of children or people with underlying health conditions. The upcoming reopening of schools in September heightens concerns, as it is expected to increase opportunities for transmission among this vulnerable group. Close contact Though the outbreak of 2022 primarily affected people with new and/or multiple sexual partners – including men who have sex with men – there are many ways to get infected. The disease primarily spreads through skin-to-skin contact with lesions, so anyone who comes into close physical contact with a contagious person is at risk. HealthConditions Discover more Health Conditions Health Geographic Reference Contact with objects used by the infected person – such as clothing and eating utensils – also represents an important source of infection. ‘Critical gaps’ in surveillance UNICEF warns that “critical gaps” in surveillance strategies – especially in identifying contacts, testing samples and tracing how the virus spread – may complicate efforts to address the outbreak. The agency is working with local authorities and partners to address them and organise information campaigns for children and families. These initiatives require more than $150,000 in flexible emergency funding. “With early detection, care, and prevention, mpox can be managed and its spread reduced,” stressed the UNICEF Deputy Representative Sandra Martins. “We urge families to seek care immediately if a child or family member develops a rash, fever, swollen lymph nodes or other symptoms, and to follow guidance on handwashing, avoiding close contact with suspected cases, and reducing stigma.” FamilyLaw #mpox outbreak # Guinea-Bissau mpox # mpox cases # mpox children # UNICEF mpox warning # mpox symptoms # mpox virus # West Africa mpox # Guinea-Bissau health # infectious disease # Public Health Support Our Journalism We cannot do without you.. your contribution supports unbiased journalism IBNS is not driven by any ism- not wokeism, not racism, not skewed secularism, not hyper right-wing or left liberal ideals, nor by any hardline religious beliefs or hyper nationalism. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Children bear brunt in Guinea-Bissau’s first mpox outbreak

News Brief
Guinea-Bissau has reported its first-ever mpox outbreak, with 46 suspected cases and seven confirmed cases as of 17 August 2026. Children under 15 make up half of the suspected cases, and 16 of these are aged 0-4 years, raising concerns due to their higher risk of severe complications. The outbreak was declared on 4 July 2026, and the reopening of schools in September could increase transmission. UNICEF is working with the Ministry of Public Health, WHO, and partners to strengthen surveillance, contact tracing, and infection prevention measures. However, gaps in community-based surveillance persist, and there is uncertainty about the full extent of the outbreak due to possible undetected transmission chains. No deaths have been reported to date, but the situation remains a major public health concern.

UNICEF is working closely with the Ministry of Public Health, WHO, and partners to support the government-led response, strengthen surveillance and contact tracing, reinforce infection prevention control and water, sanitation and hygiene measures in health facilities, and ensure that children and families receive timely, and accurate and information to help prevent further spread of mpox. “With early detection, care, and prevention, mpox can be managed and its spread reduced,” said Sandra Martins. “We urge families to seek care immediately if a child or family member develops a rash, fever, swollen lymph nodes or other symptoms, and to follow guidance on handwashing, avoiding close contact with suspected cases, and reducing stigma.” While the broader national response is underway, UNICEF requires more than US$150,000 to support targeted, critical pillars of the mpox intervention in Guinea-Bissau. Flexible emergency funding is urgently required to deploy these vital resources and protect vulnerable populations. WN/as/APA Share. Time
The outbreak was declared on 4 July 2026, and as of 17 August 2026, seven confirmed mpox cases have been reported. The publication date is 20 August 2026.
Location and population
Guinea-Bissau, specifically the Autonomous Sector of Bissau and Biombo Region. Children under 15 account for half of the 46 suspected cases, with 23 being children and 16 aged 0-4 years.
Event
Guinea-Bissau experienced its first mpox outbreak, with 46 suspected cases and seven confirmed cases. The event is confirmed by the Ministry of Public Health and UNICEF, with the outbreak being a major public health concern.
Scale, impact and risk
There are 46 suspected mpox cases, with seven confirmed. The confirmed cases are all among adults, while children under 15 account for half of the suspected cases. The risk is heightened due to the vulnerability of young children to severe complications and the potential for increased transmission with the reopening of schools in September.
Response, status and uncertainty
UNICEF is collaborating with the Ministry of Public Health, WHO, and partners to support the government-led response, including strengthening surveillance, contact tracing, and infection prevention. However, critical gaps remain in community-based surveillance, and there is uncertainty about the full extent of the outbreak due to possible undetected transmission chains.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

RI health experts warn of uptick in mpox, but risk still low to public

News Brief
Rhode Island health experts have warned of an uptick in mpox cases, with at least six reported in a local clinic over the past several weeks. This marks an unusual increase after many months without cases. The Rhode Island Department of Health has issued a professional advisory urging caregivers to stay alert for mpox and seek medical attention if they notice something out of the ordinary on their body. Philip Chan, a physician and chief medical officer at the Rhode Island Public Health Institute, noted that the vaccine can reduce symptoms if administered within four days of exposure. The risk to the general public is described as low, though there is a noted impact among gay, bisexual, or other men who have sex with men. The mpox vaccine is given in two doses 28 days apart, and those already vaccinated do not need to receive it again. The situation is being monitored, and healthcare professionals are emphasizing the importance of early detection and response.

WPRI ProvidenceFollow RI health experts warn of uptick in mpox, but risk still low to public Leah Crowley Thu, August 20, 2026 at 8:30 PM UTC 2 min read Add Yahoo as a preferred source to see more of our stories on Google. (WPRI) — Health experts are warning of an uptick in mpox cases after a local clinic reported seeing at least six cases in the past several weeks. Dr. Philip Chan, a physician and the chief medical officer at the Rhode Island Public Health Institute and Open Door Health, said the state has "definitely seen a handful" of mpox cases. "That's unusual because there's been no cases for many months," Chan said. Advertisement Advertisement According to the World Health Organization, people with mpox often get a rash or other symptoms like a fever, muscle aches, sore throat or swollen lymph nodes within 1-21 days of being exposed to the virus. "We do see, more impact cases, unfortunately, among gay, bisexual or other men who have sex with men," Chan explained. "Certainly if you identify as part of the LGBTQ community ... make sure to look into getting vaccinated." The mpox vaccine is given in two doses 28 days apart. Chan noted that people who have already been vaccinated for mpox do not need to get the vaccine again. "Sometimes [mpox is] very painful, and so the vaccine, if it doesn't prevent it completely, will reduce the number of symptoms, reduce the number of lesions, reduce the rash," Chan said. Advertisement Advertisement Michaela Maynard, a nurse practitioner and senior director of clinical operations at Open Door Health, noted that there are steps people can take if they've been exposed to the virus. "We also know that we can use vaccines once someone has been exposed," Maynard said. "So, if there is a person who might have been exposed to someone with mpox, and it's been within four days of that exposure, you can actually get vaccinated and that can help prevent it." According to the institute, the Rhode Island Department of Health issued a professional advisory surrounding the uptick in cases, urging caregivers to "stay alert for mpox." If you notice something out of the ordinary on your body, you should seek medical attention. Advertisement Advertisement "We definitely always recommend that they have a clinician or provider take a look at it," Maynard said. B routine priorityPublic-health news

How To Tell The Difference Between Monkeypox And Chickenpox - AOL.ca

News Brief
This article, published on August 21, 2026, by AOL.ca, provides a comparison between monkeypox and chickenpox. It clarifies that while the two diseases share similar symptoms, they are not related. According to the CDC, monkeypox is more closely related to the smallpox virus, but it spreads through direct contact, including skin-to-skin contact and respiratory secretions. The article notes that most reported cases of monkeypox in the current outbreak have been contracted through sexual contact. It highlights that monkeypox symptoms can last two to four weeks, and while complications such as pneumonia or encephalitis are possible, no deaths have been reported from the current outbreak. The Cleveland Clinic assures that existing vaccines are effective at preventing monkeypox. The article also emphasizes prevention measures, including vaccination, frequent handwashing, and avoiding close contact with infected individuals. It advises readers to consult a doctor if they suspect either infection and to cover their bumps and wear a mask when visiting the doctor.
Original source text (English)
If you're over a certain age, you or someone you know probably had chickenpox as a kid, and unless you've been living under a rock, you've probably heard about monkeypox by now. While the two have similar names, they are actually not related. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), monkeypox is more closely related to the virus that causes smallpox. Monkeypox symptoms are generally milder than those of smallpox, though the infection can still cause death in some cases. Existing vaccines are effective at preventing infection (per CDC ). Chickenpox is a varicella-zoster virus (VZV) (per CDC ). While highly contagious, and extremely irritating, chickenpox is rare today, thanks to the vaccine made available in the U.S. in 1995. In some cases, chickenpox can cause serious complications, including bacterial infections, pneumonia, encephalitis, hemorrhage, or sepsis. People with monkeypox can also develop pneumonia or encephalitis, but no deaths have been reported from the current outbreak of monkeypox, assures the Cleveland Clinic . Some symptoms of chickenpox and monkeypox can appear similar, but there are apparent distinguishing factors. Here's how to tell the difference between monkeypox and chickenpox. Read more: What Most People Say Before They Die, According To Top Palliative Doctor, Simran Malhotra - Exclusive Symptoms and prevention The CDC says monkeypox spreads through direct contact, which is often in the form of skin-to-skin contact, but sometimes through contact with respiratory secretions of, or objects used by, someone with the infection. It can also be contracted by being bitten or scratched by, or preparing or eating the meat of, infected animals. Most reported cases of monkeypox in the current outbreak have been contracted through sexual contact, according to UNC Health Talk . Contagion begins as soon as symptoms appear and lasts until the rash disappears entirely and a new layer of skin has formed. Symptoms can last two to four weeks. Chickenpox also spreads through close contact but can easily be contracted without sexual contact (via CDC ). Illness typically lasts between four to seven days, according to the CDC . People with both chickenpox and monkeypox infections develop blisters on the skin. However, monkeypox often causes swollen lymph nodes, while chickenpox typically does not, says the Cleveland Clinic . Per the CDC , monkeypox infection can be prevented by getting a vaccine if you are at risk or have been exposed, washing your hands often, and avoiding close contact with people who have rashes that resemble monkeypox, as well as the objects they touch. Vaccines are also available to prevent chickenpox . Prevention notes that if you believe you may have either chickenpox or monkeypox, you should call your doctor and ask about getting tested. Be sure to cover your bumps and wear a mask when visiting the doctor. Curious about health? Add Health Digest to your preferred sources for daily insights! Read the original article on Health Digest

The article was published on 2026-08-21. The event date is not explicitly mentioned in the evidence, but the current outbreak of monkeypox is referenced.

The article is general in nature and does not specify a particular country, region, community, or facility. It discusses monkeypox and chickenpox in a broad context.
Event
The article explains the differences between monkeypox and chickenpox, noting that they are not related and highlighting symptoms, transmission, and prevention methods. It is reported by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) and the Cleveland Clinic.
Scale, impact and risk
Monkeypox symptoms are generally milder than those of smallpox, though the infection can still cause death in some cases. However, no deaths have been reported from the current outbreak of monkeypox. Both infections can cause complications such as pneumonia or encephalitis.
Response, status and uncertainty
The article provides general prevention and testing guidance, including vaccination, handwashing, and avoiding close contact with infected individuals. It also advises consulting a doctor if symptoms are suspected.

文章报告了相关情况,但未提及任何具体调查、检测、隔离、密切接触者追踪、预防或治疗措施。暴发的状态仍处于调查阶段。

Mpox: Children account for half of suspected cases in Guinea-Bissau - Welcome to the United Nations

News Brief
The United Nations reported on 20 August 2026 that children account for half of the 46 suspected mpox cases in Guinea-Bissau, with 16 of them under the age of four. The article emphasizes the vulnerability of children to the disease but does not provide details on the investigation, testing, or response measures. The outbreak is described as under investigation, and no explicit risk level or health-system impact is mentioned. The information is based on the current status of suspected cases, highlighting the need for further analysis and action to address the situation in Guinea-Bissau.

猴痘解读

Monkeypox explainer
新闻简报猴痘是由猴痘病毒引起的疾病,该病毒是引起天花的天花病毒的近缘病原体。猴痘与天花感染可引发相似症状,但猴痘的临床表现通常较轻。

Monkeypox explainer

News Brief
Monkeypox is a disease caused by the monkeypox virus, a pathogen closely related to variola virus, which causes smallpox. Monkeypox and smallpox infections trigger similar symptoms, but monkeypox.
